# 1. OBSTETRIC ANESTHESIA: ENHANCED NOTES WITH ASA PRACTICE GUIDELINES
<!-- EPA-6:K13:obstetric_icu EPA-6:EQ1:obstetric_critical_care -->

**Source Integration**: These enhanced notes incorporate the 2016 ASA Practice Guidelines for Obstetric Anesthesia and the 2023 ASA Statement on Pain During Cesarean Delivery, providing evidence-based recommendations alongside comprehensive educational content. This integration ensures comprehensive coverage of both routine obstetric anesthesia care and specialized management of intraoperative pain during cesarean delivery.

# 2. PERIANESTHETIC EVALUATION AND PREPARATION
<!-- EPA-7:K1:preoperative_assessment EPA-7:S1:clinical_assessment_skills -->

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|Part B: Obstetrics]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: Medical Knowledge]]

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Perianesthetic Evaluation and Preparation

## 2.1. HISTORY AND PHYSICAL EXAMINATION

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: History and Physical Examination

### 2.1.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Conduct a focused history and physical examination before providing anesthesia care**:
  * Should include maternal health and anesthetic history
  * Relevant obstetric history
  * Baseline blood pressure measurement
  * Airway, heart, and lung examination consistent with ASA Practice Advisory for Preanesthesia Evaluation
  * When neuraxial anesthetic planned, examine patient's back
* **Recognition of significant anesthetic or obstetric risk factors should encourage consultation** between obstetrician and anesthesiologist
* **Communication system should be in place** to encourage early and ongoing contact between obstetric providers, anesthesiologists, and multidisciplinary team members

### 2.1.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** Observational studies suggest certain patient characteristics (hypertensive disorders, obesity, diabetes) may be associated with obstetric complications (Category B2/B3-H evidence).

**Survey Findings:** Consultants and ASA members strongly agree to conduct focused history and physical examination and maintain multidisciplinary communication systems.

### 2.1.3. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* **Risk factor identification** for:
  * Difficult airway (obesity, preeclampsia with facial edema)
  * Hemorrhagic complications (prior uterine surgery, placental abnormalities)
  * Cardiovascular compromise (cardiac disease, severe preeclampsia)
  * Coagulation disorders (HELLP syndrome, anticoagulation therapy)
* **Documentation requirements** for medico-legal protection and continuity of care
* **Early consultation** facilitates optimal delivery planning and resource allocation

## 2.2. INTRAPARTUM PLATELET COUNT

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Intrapartum Platelet Count

### 2.2.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Anesthesiologist's decision to order or require platelet count should be individualized** based on:
  * Patient history (e.g., preeclampsia with severe features)
  * Physical examination findings
  * Clinical signs suggesting coagulopathy
* **Routine platelet count not necessary** in healthy parturient

### 2.2.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** 
* Literature insufficient to assess whether routine platelet count predicts anesthesia-related complications in uncomplicated parturients
* Observational studies suggest platelet count and fibrinogen values associated with postpartum hemorrhage frequency (Category B2 evidence)
* Studies suggest platelet count useful for diagnosing hypertensive disorders and conditions associated with coagulopathy (Category B3/B4-B evidence)

**Survey Findings:** Consultants and ASA members strongly agree that platelet count decisions should be individualized based on history, examination, and clinical signs.

### 2.2.3. CLINICAL INDICATIONS FOR PLATELET COUNT

* **Hypertensive disorders:**
  * Preeclampsia with severe features
  * HELLP syndrome (hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, low platelets)
  * Eclampsia
* **Other high-risk conditions:**
  * Suspected disseminated intravascular coagulation
  * Anticoagulant therapy
  * History of bleeding disorders
  * Clinical signs of coagulopathy

## 2.3. BLOOD TYPE AND SCREEN

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Blood Type and Screen

### 2.3.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Routine blood cross-match not necessary** for healthy and uncomplicated parturients for vaginal or operative delivery
* **Decision to order blood type and screen or cross-match should be individualized** based on:
  * Maternal history
  * Anticipated hemorrhagic complications (e.g., placenta accreta in patient with placenta previa and previous uterine surgery)
  * Local institutional policies

### 2.3.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** Literature insufficient to determine whether obtaining blood type and screen or cross-match is associated with fewer maternal anesthetic complications.

**Survey Findings:** ASA members agree and consultants strongly agree that routine cross-match unnecessary for uncomplicated deliveries, but decisions should be individualized.

### 2.3.3. RISK STRATIFICATION FOR BLOOD PRODUCT PREPARATION

* **Low risk (Type and Screen sufficient):**
  * Uncomplicated vaginal delivery
  * Uncomplicated cesarean delivery
  * No significant maternal comorbidities
* **Intermediate risk (Consider Cross-match):**
  * Multiple prior uterine surgeries
  * Known placental abnormalities
  * Maternal bleeding disorders
* **High risk (Cross-match recommended):**
  * Placenta accreta spectrum
  * Placenta previa with prior cesarean delivery
  * Anticipated complex surgery

## 2.4. PERIANESTHETIC FETAL HEART RATE MONITORING

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Perianesthetic Recording of Fetal Heart Rate Patterns

### 2.4.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Fetal heart rate patterns should be monitored by qualified individual** before and after administration of neuraxial analgesia for labor
* **Continuous electronic recording may not be necessary** in every clinical setting and may not be possible during placement of neuraxial catheter

### 2.4.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** Observational studies and case reports indicate fetal heart rate patterns may change after administration of neuraxial anesthetics (Category B3/B4 evidence).

**Survey Findings:** Consultants and ASA members strongly agree that qualified individual should monitor fetal heart rate patterns before and after neuraxial analgesia.

### 2.4.3. PRACTICAL CONSIDERATIONS

* **Pre-procedure monitoring** establishes baseline fetal status
* **Post-procedure monitoring** detects potential complications:
  * Maternal hypotension affecting uteroplacental perfusion
  * Uterine hyperstimulation (rare with epidural opioids)
  * Local anesthetic systemic toxicity effects
* **Documentation** of fetal status supports clinical decision-making
* **Communication** with obstetric team regarding any concerning changes

**Bottom Line:** Perianesthetic evaluation should include focused history and physical examination, with individualized decisions regarding laboratory studies based on patient risk factors rather than routine protocols. Effective communication systems between anesthesia and obstetric teams optimize patient safety. Fetal heart rate monitoring before and after neuraxial procedures helps detect potential complications while recognizing that continuous monitoring during procedure placement may not always be feasible.

## 2.5. ENHANCED PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT FOR CESAREAN DELIVERY

**ASA Statement Reference**: Statement on Pain During Cesarean Delivery - Preoperative Assessment

### 2.5.1. IMPORTANCE OF SHARED DECISION-MAKING

* **Collaborative approach**:
  * Shared decision-making process explores risks, benefits, and alternatives to treatment options `ASA Statement 2023`.
  * Benefits include fewer patient regrets, better perceived communication, improved treatment adherence, increased confidence and coping skills `ASA Statement 2023`.
  * Leads to better health outcomes when multiple management options are possible `ASA Statement 2023`.

### 2.5.2. AWARENESS OF PAIN DURING CESAREAN DELIVERY

* **Prevalence and underestimation**:
  * True prevalence of pain during cesarean section may be up to 20% or more `ASA Statement 2023`.
  * Significant under-estimation may occur as studies focus on anesthesia interventions rather than self-reported pain `ASA Statement 2023`.
  * In prospective studies, failure to achieve pain-free surgery occurred in 6% with spinal anesthesia, 18% with combined spinal-epidural, 24% with labor epidural conversion `ASA Statement 2023`.

### 2.5.3. RISK FACTOR IDENTIFICATION

* **Patient-specific factors** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * High BMI
  * History of spine surgery
  * History of pain during prior cesarean delivery
  * Fear of pain
  * Chronic pain conditions
  * Opioid use disorder
* **Neuraxial procedure factors** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Neuraxial administration at the level of L5-S1
* **Labor-specific factors** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Intrapartum breakthrough pain
  * Increased pain scores within 2 hours of cesarean
  * Chorioamnionitis
* **Obstetric factors** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Urgent/emergent cesarean delivery
  * Expected duration of cesarean
  * Planned exteriorization of uterus

### 2.5.4. TRAUMA-INFORMED PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT

* **Historical considerations** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Identify patients with history of traumatic delivery
  * Previous pain during cesarean delivery
  * Stress in pregnancy and anxiety
  * Past medical or non-medical traumatic experiences
* **Communication approach** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Ask about concerns and fears using compassionate and respectful communication
  * Acknowledge that pain during and after cesarean delivery is a primary patient concern
  * Create supportive environment for patient involvement in care decisions
  * Use three-talk model: team talk, option talk, decision talk

### 2.5.5. PATIENT PREFERENCES AND EXPECTATIONS

* **Setting realistic expectations** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Discuss potential for pain and discomfort during cesarean delivery
  * When given choice, patients were willing to tolerate pain score of 56 ± 22 out of 100 on visual analog scale before taking pain medication
  * Acknowledge potential for pain during cesarean section in preoperative consent to build trust and open communication
* **Values assessment**:
  * Seek understanding of patient preferences for pain management
  * Balance pain relief versus risk preferences and side effects
  * Consider impact on breastfeeding when discussing pain medication options

### 2.5.6. DOWNSTREAM CONSEQUENCES AWARENESS

* **Psychological impact** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Significant pain during cesarean may affect patient experience
  * Associated as independent risk factor for postpartum post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD)
  * In UK, pain during cesarean delivery was most common cause for litigation related to obstetric anesthesia care
* **Long-term effects**:
  * Perinatal pain significantly increased risk of postpartum depression (OR 1.43)
  * Patient perception important predictor for pain consequences
  * Communication and support encouraged to mitigate negative outcomes

### 2.5.7. HEALTH DISPARITIES AWARENESS

* **Documented disparities in anesthesia care** `ASA Statement 2023`:
  * Disparities in type of anesthesia used for cesarean delivery and maternal pain management occur
  * African-American race (aOR 1.7-1.9) and Hispanic ethnicity (aOR 1.5) had significantly increased association for receiving general anesthesia
  * Disparities persist even after adjustment for obstetric and non-obstetric covariates
* **Mitigation strategies**:
  * Recognition of risk factors and past traumatic experiences
  * Patient education and shared decision-making processes
  * Cultural competency in communication approaches
  * Systematic efforts to reduce disparities in pain management and anesthetic care

**Bottom Line:** Enhanced preoperative assessment for cesarean delivery should incorporate awareness of pain prevalence (up to 20%), systematic risk factor identification, trauma-informed communication, and shared decision-making. Recognition of patient concerns about pain, setting realistic expectations, and building trust through open communication can improve patient experience and reduce risk of adverse psychological outcomes including PTSD and postpartum depression.

# 3. ASPIRATION PREVENTION

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|Part B: Obstetrics]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: Medical Knowledge]]

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Aspiration Prevention

## 3.1. CLEAR LIQUIDS

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Clear Liquids

### 3.1.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Oral intake of moderate amounts of clear liquids may be allowed** for uncomplicated laboring patients
* **Uncomplicated patients undergoing elective surgery** may have clear liquids up to 2 hours before induction of anesthesia
* **Examples of clear liquids** include water, fruit juices without pulp, carbonated beverages, clear tea, black coffee, and sports drinks
* **Volume is less important than presence of particulate matter** in liquid ingested
* **Patients with additional risk factors** (morbid obesity, diabetes, difficult airway) or increased risk for operative delivery may have further restrictions determined case-by-case

### 3.1.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** Insufficient published literature to examine relation between fasting times for clear liquids and risk of emesis/reflux or pulmonary aspiration during labor.

**Survey Findings:** ASA members agree and consultants strongly agree regarding clear liquid guidelines for laboring and elective surgery patients.

### 3.1.3. CLINICAL CONSIDERATIONS

* **Risk factors requiring modified approach:**
  * Morbid obesity (BMI ≥40 kg/m²)
  * Diabetes mellitus (delayed gastric emptying)
  * Difficult airway anatomy
  * Nonreassuring fetal heart rate pattern
  * Multiple gestations
  * History of gastroesophageal reflux disease

## 3.2. SOLIDS

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Solids

### 3.2.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Solid foods should be avoided in laboring patients**
* **Patients undergoing elective surgery** should undergo fasting period for solids of 6-8 hours depending on type of food ingested (fat content)
* **Laboring patients with additional risk factors** may have further restrictions of oral intake

### 3.2.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:** 
* Specific fasting time for solids predictive of maternal anesthetic complications has not been determined
* Insufficient literature to address safety of any particular fasting period for solids in obstetric patients

**Survey Findings:** Consultants and ASA members strongly agree on solid food fasting recommendations.

### 3.2.3. RATIONALE AND IMPLEMENTATION

* **Solid food considerations:**
  * Fat content affects gastric emptying time
  * High-fat meals require longer fasting periods
  * Individual patient factors may require extended fasting
* **Emergency situations:** All pregnant women after first trimester considered to have full stomachs regardless of fasting status

## 3.3. PHARMACOLOGIC ASPIRATION PROPHYLAXIS

**ASA Guidelines Reference**: Antacids, H2-receptor Antagonists, and Metoclopramide

### 3.3.1. ASA RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Before surgical procedures** (cesarean delivery, postpartum tubal ligation), consider timely administration of:
  * Nonparticulate antacids
  * H2-receptor antagonists  
  * Metoclopramide
* **For aspiration prophylaxis** as indicated by clinical circumstances

### 3.3.2. EVIDENCE BASE

**Literature Findings:**
* **Nonparticulate antacids:** Associated with higher gastric pH values during peripartum period (Category A2-B evidence), equivocal regarding gastric volume (Category A2-E evidence)
* **H2-receptor antagonists:** Associated with higher gastric pH in obstetric patients (Category A2-B evidence), equivocal regarding gastric volume (Category A2-E evidence)  
* **Metoclopramide:** Associated with reduced peripartum nausea and vomiting (Category A2-B evidence)
* **Limitation:** Literature not available examining relation between reduced gastric acidity and frequency of pulmonary aspiration, emesis, morbidity, or mortality

**Survey Findings:** Consultants and ASA members agree on consideration of pharmacologic prophylaxis before surgical procedures.

### 3.3.3. SPECIFIC AGENTS AND DOSING

* **Nonparticulate antacids:**
  * Sodium citrate 15-30 mL (0.3 M solution)
  * Sodium bicarbonate
  * **Timing:** Immediately before induction for emergency cases
* **H2-receptor antagonists:**
  * Ranitidine 150 mg PO/50 mg IV
  * Famotidine 20 mg PO/20 mg IV
  * **Timing:** 1-2 hours before procedure for optimal effect
* **Metoclopramide:**
  * 10 mg IV/IM
  * **Benefits:** Promotes gastric emptying, antiemetic effect
  * **Contraindications:** Gastrointestinal obstruction, pheochromocytoma

### 3.3.4. ENHANCED ASPIRATION PREVENTION STRATEGIES

* **High-risk patients:** Consider combination therapy
* **Timing optimization:** Coordinate administration with anticipated delivery timing
* **Emergency protocols:** Immediate availability of aspiration prophylaxis agents
* **Patient education:** Regarding NPO guidelines and rationale

**Bottom Line:** Aspiration prevention in obstetric patients requires individualized approach to NPO guidelines and pharmacologic prophylaxis. Clear liquids may be allowed for uncomplicated laboring patients up to 2 hours before elective procedures, while solid foods should be avoided during labor. Risk factors such as obesity, diabetes, and difficult airway may require modified restrictions. Pharmacologic prophylaxis with nonparticulate antacids, H2-receptor antagonists, and metoclopramide should be considered before surgical procedures, with evidence supporting their effectiveness in reducing gastric acidity and nausea/vomiting.

# 4. ANATOMIC AND PHYSIOLOGIC CHANGES OF PREGNANCY
<!-- EPA-7:K1:physiological_changes_pregnancy EPA-7:K4:applied_anatomy_obstetric_RA -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|Part A: Physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|Part B: Obstetrics]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: Medical Knowledge]]

## 4.1. AIRWAY AND PULMONARY CHANGES

### 4.1.1. ANATOMICAL CHANGES

* **Mucosal edema and engorgement** of nasal, oropharyngeal, and laryngeal tissues throughout pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]]:
  * ⚠️ More prominent at birth due to increased extracellular fluid (increases by 3L at term) and hormonal changes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Can worsen in parturients with preeclampsia, after pushing during second stage of labor, or with tocolytic therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Insertion of nasal devices should be done carefully to minimize bleeding risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Mallampati scores** tend to increase throughout pregnancy and labor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Breast engorgement** may make tracheal intubation more difficult [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Short-handled laryngoscope is recommended when tracheal intubation is needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Pregnancy-related capillary engorgement** causes oedema of the oropharyngeal mucosa and larynx [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * In pre-eclampsia, the change in capillary dynamics can significantly worsen airway oedema [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
* Subcostal angle increases from 68 degrees to 103 degrees at term due to relaxin, secreted by corpus luteum.

### 4.1.2. RESPIRATORY PHYSIOLOGY CHANGES

* **Hormonal effects**:
  * Increased progesterone causes bronchodilation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Progesterone stimulates the respiratory centre in the medulla from early pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
* **Ventilatory changes**:
  * Respiratory rate increases slightly (10%) in the 1st and 2nd trimester but plateaus in the 3rd trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Tidal volume (TV) increases by approximately 45% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Minute ventilation (V̇E) increases by 50% at term and can remain elevated until 6-8 weeks postpartum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Alveolar ventilation increases by 70% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * V̇E increases further in labour due to pain [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Anatomical dead space increases due to bronchodilatation from progesterone-induced smooth muscle relaxation by 45% (but Vd/Vt remains same) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
* **Lung volumes** (see figure in source material):
  * Functional residual capacity (FRC) decreases by up to 30% at term due to diaphragmatic splinting [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
	  * Consequent apneic time at 100% pre-oxygenation is around 2-3min instead of 8-9min
	  * 20% decrease in upright position, drops to 70% in supine
  * Residual volume (RV) decreases by approximately 25% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Expiratory reserve volume (ERV) decreases by approximately 20% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Closing volume and closing capacity remain unchanged during pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Minimal decrease in total lung capacity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1), forced vital capacity (FVC), FEV1/FVC ratio, and dead space remain relatively unchanged [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * FRC is reduced by 20% when standing and by a further 30% in the supine position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Vital capacity remains unchanged [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Lung compliance is unchanged, but total respiratory compliance is decreased.

| Parameter       | Change    | Percentage |
| --------------- | --------- | ---------- |
| **Increased**   |           |            |
| MV + Alv V      | Increased | 45-50%     |
| Vt              | Increased | 40-45%     |
| RR              | Increased | 10%        |
| Insp Cap        | Increased | 10-15%     |
| Vd (anatomical) | Increased | 45%        |
| **Decreased**   |           |            |
| Raw             | Decreased | 35%        |
| Total resp comp | Decreased | 20%        |
| FRC             | Decreased | 20%        |
| ERV             | Decreased | 20%        |
| RV              | Decreased | 15-20%     |
| EC              | Decreased | 20%        |
| TLC             | Decreased | 5%         |
| PVR             | Decreased | 50%        |
| **Unchanged**   |           |            |
| Lung compliance | Unchanged | -          |
| VC              | Unchanged | -          |
![[Pasted image 20250714145222.png]]
### 4.1.3. RESPIRATORY FUNCTION

* **Oxygen consumption**:
  * Increases by up to 21% during pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Increases dramatically during labor:
	* 45% increase in first stage [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
	* 75% increase in second stage (especially without neuraxial analgesia) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * O₂ requirements of the growing foetus result in a 20% increase in O₂ consumption at term [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * O₂ consumption is further increased during labor due to uterine contractions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .
  * Increased 2,3 DPG shifts O2-Hb curve to the right (decreased affinity to Hb)
* **Acid-base changes**:
  * Pregnancy creates a state of incompletely compensated respiratory alkalosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * pH ranges between 7.42-7.46 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * PaCO₂ decreases to approximately 30 mmHg at term [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * PaO₂ increases from approximately 93 mmHg to 106 mmHg [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]].
  * Mild respiratory alkalosis triggers a compensatory renal HCO₃⁻ loss (typical plasma HCO₃⁻ concentration is 20 mmol/L) - ph ~7.44 [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|ⓘ]] .

### 4.1.4. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* **Ventilation management**:
  * ⚠️ Apnea is poorly tolerated due to increased oxygen consumption and decreased FRC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Early desaturation during rapid sequence induction of general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Enhanced maternal uptake and elimination of inhalational anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Reduced MAC requirement (32-40%) for inhalational agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Preoxygenation less effective due to increased O₂ consumption and reduced FRC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Implications for general anesthesia** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] 
  * Special attention should be paid to maintain appropriate V̇E, aiming for a PaCO₂ of ~4.3 kPa (normal for pregnancy) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
  * Maternal respiratory acidosis can cause foetal acidosis as the foetus cannot correct a pH disturbance by respiratory or renal compensation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
  * Maternal alkalosis should be avoided as the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve is shifted to the left, reducing O₂ transfer to the foetus with the potential for foetal hypoxia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
* **Airway management** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]]
<!-- EPA-4:K1:obstetric_difficult_airway EPA-4:S1:obstetric_airway_assessment -->
  * The incidence of difficult airway in the obstetric population is around 1 in 250, eight times higher than the general population [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
  * Difficult airway may be due to:
	* Airway oedema
	* Increased mucosal vascularity (repeated attempts at laryngoscopy may result in laceration and bleeding)
	* Weight gain (large breasts and short neck make laryngoscopy more challenging)
	* Poorly applied cricoid pressure
  * Preparation is key:
	* Good head, neck and shoulder position using standard pillows or specially designed elevation pillow
	* Adequate pre-oxygenation over at least 3 minutes is essential
	* Experienced assistant with a range of ready-prepared airway equipment

**Bottom Line:** Airway edema and engorgement, increased minute ventilation, decreased FRC, and higher oxygen consumption create significant challenges for airway management and ventilation in pregnant patients. Respiratory changes begin early but are most pronounced in the third trimester. Early desaturation during apnea and airway difficulties mandate careful planning for general anesthesia, including thorough airway assessment, optimal positioning, and adequate preoxygenation.

## 4.2. CARDIOVASCULAR CHANGES

### 4.2.1. HEMODYNAMIC CHANGES

* **Cardiac output (CO)** increased by 30-50% during pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]]:
  * Begins in first trimester: 30% increase by end of first trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Peaks at end of second trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Further increases during labor:
	* 40-50% additional increase during second stage labor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
	* Highest during uterine contractions and immediately post-delivery (60-80% due to auto-transfusion and increased venous return due to uterine involution) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Returns to prelabor levels within hours after delivery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Returns to prepregnancy values within 2 weeks after delivery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Factors contributing to CO increase [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] 
	* Reduction in afterload: Systemic vascular resistance (SVR) falls by 20% due to progesterone-induced vasodilatation
	* Increased heart rate: 25% increase over the course of pregnancy
	* Increased preload: Increased circulating blood volume results in increase in cardiac preload resulting from increased aldosterone
	* Myocardial contractility is unchanged by pregnancy
* **Stroke volume** increases by 20-50% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]]:
  * Peaks at end of second trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Increases by 30%, with much of the increase occurring in the first trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .
* **Heart rate** increases by 15-25%, decreasing rapidly post-delivery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Blood pressure (BP)** changes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]]:
  * Systolic BP, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and diastolic BP all decrease early in pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * BP begins to increase during third trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Returns to prepregnancy levels postpartum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Systolic blood pressure falls by 5% and diastolic blood pressure falls by 10% due to reduced SVR [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .
* **Systemic vascular resistance (SVR)** decreases by 20-50% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]]:
  * Begins as early as 8 weeks gestation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
  * Due to peripheral vasodilation from progesterone, relaxin, and other hormones [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
- **Risk of LV failure and APO**
### 4.2.2. CARDIAC STRUCTURE AND FUNCTION

* **Left ventricular hypertrophy** (50% increase in mass at term) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Diastolic dysfunction** (reversible) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Heart displacement** cranially and to the left (cephalad to 4th intercostal space and left to MCL) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Heart sounds**: 3rd HS, benign flow murmur (systolic LSE), continuous murmur (venous flow)
* **ECG changes** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]]:
  * Left axis deviation
  * ST depression
  * Flattened T waves
  * TWI III/V1-3
  * Tachycardia
  * Shortened PR + QT intervals
* **TTE**: LVH, valvular regurgitation (except aortic annulus)
- Changes are fully reversible
### 4.2.3. AORTOCAVAL COMPRESSION

* Occurs after second trimester (usually from **20th week**) when supine [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* Near complete occlusion of vena cava at term [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* Results in decreased preload and fall in stroke volume and CO by approximately 25% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* ⚠️ Can lead to significant maternal hypotension, compensatory tachycardia, nausea, altered mental status, and decreased uterine perfusion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* 💡 Management: Left uterine displacement to allow the uterus to shift away from the great vessels [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* Aortocaval compression begins from 20 weeks' gestation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] 
  * Reduced maternal venous return leads to:
	* Decreased cardiac preload causing fall in CO via a **reverse Bainbridge reflex**
	* Symptoms including nausea, pallor, hypotension or cardiovascular collapse when supine
	* Symptoms resolve in lateral position where IVC compression is relieved
  * Reduced placental blood flow:
	* Blood flow to placenta is not autoregulated; directly proportional to perfusion pressure
	* Compression of descending aorta further reduces uteroplacental blood flow
	* May result in inadequate foetal gas exchange
* Compensation mechanisms in non-anaesthetised parturients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] 
  * Increased sympathetic outflow, which increases SVR and HR
  * Blood bypass of compressed IVC through collateral pathways: azygos, paravertebral and epidural veins
* ⚠️ General anesthesia and neuraxial blockade abolish the sympathetic response to aortocaval compression; severe hypotension or cardiac arrest may develop rapidly in the supine position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .
* Aortocaval compression is usually relieved by left ateral tilt **on the right side** (operating table or wedge) but is guaranteed only in the full lateral position [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .
* Lateral tilt with a wedge is especially important during cardiopulmonary resuscitation of pregnant patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .

![[Pasted image 20250714150254.png]]
### 4.2.4. OTHER CARDIOVASCULAR CHANGES

* **Uterine blood flow** increases dramatically from 50-100 mL/min to 700-900 mL/min at term [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Renal blood flow** increases by 60% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* Decreased responsiveness to endogenous and exogenous vasoconstrictors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* Central venous pressure and pulmonary capillary wedge pressure remain relatively unchanged [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]].
* **Blood volume** increases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] 
  * Total blood volume increases by 40% for singleton pregnancy
  * Red cell mass increases by 20-30% due to increased erythropoietin secretion
  * Plasma volume increases by 45% due to oestrogenic stimulation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system
  * Discrepancy between increases leads to physiological anemia of pregnancy (Hb falls to around 120 g/L at term)
  * Each uterine contraction squeezes 300-500 mL of blood from the uterus to systemic circulation during labor
  * Blood loss at delivery is typically 300 mL for vaginal delivery and 500 mL for cesarean section
* During labor, CO is increased further (25-50%) in response to catecholamine secretion and increased further by 20-30% during uterine contractions [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .
* Following delivery, the autotransfusion of 500 mL of uterine blood to the venous system increases cardiac preload, resulting in a 60-80% increase in CO - a moment of great danger to parturients at risk of cardiac failure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.1 Cardiovascular System|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy creates a hyperdynamic circulatory state with increased cardiac output, heart rate, and stroke volume, while systemic vascular resistance decreases. These changes begin in the first trimester and peak in the second trimester. Aortocaval compression by the gravid uterus is a critical consideration after 20 weeks, requiring lateral positioning during anesthesia. The immediate postpartum period presents a period of significant hemodynamic stress due to autotransfusion from the contracted uterus.

| Parameter     | Change    | Percentage              |
| ------------- | --------- | ----------------------- |
| **Increased** |           |                         |
| CO            | Increased | 50%                     |
| SV            | Increased | 35%                     |
| HR            | Increased | 25%                     |
| **Decreased** |           |                         |
| SVR           | Decreased | 30%                     |
| SBP/DBP       | Decreased | 10%                     |
|               |           | _(DBP decreases > SBP)_ |
| **Unchanged** |           |                         |
| CVP           | Unchanged | -                       |
| PCWP          | Unchanged | -                       |
## 4.3. HEMATOLOGY AND COAGULATION

### 4.3.1. BLOOD VOLUME CHANGES

* **Total blood volume** increases by 40% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Creates a state of relative volume overload [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Increases from 6 weeks, peaks at 34 weeks, returns to normal 10-14 days after birth [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Physiological hypervolaemia protects from hypotension, increases nutrient delivery to fetus, reduces blood loss and hematocrit, reduces viscosity and increases patency of uteroplacental bed
* **Plasma volume** increases by 40-50% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Due to substantial activation of renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system by extrogen [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Leads to increased mineralocorticoid activity and sodium/water retention [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 4.3.2. RED BLOOD CELLS AND HEMOGLOBIN

* **Red blood cell mass** increases by approximately 20% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Lags compared to plasma volume increase [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Results in relative anemia of pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Hemoglobin** levels may be as low as 11 g/dL (normal in pregnancy) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Relative anemia peaks at 32-34 weeks gestation (plasma volume increase > RBC volume) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Renal EPO production increases, but not enough to match plasma volume expansion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 4.3.3. COAGULATION SYSTEM

* **Procoagulant changes**:
  * All procoagulant factor levels increase except factors XI and XIII [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Fibrinogen concentration markedly increased to 400-500 mg/dL or higher [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Acute decreases in fibrinogen concentration are an early predictor of postpartum hemorrhage severity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Hypercoagulable state is established with increased fibrinogen and clotting factors VII, X and XII [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Anticoagulant changes**:
  * Decreased anticoagulant activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
	* Decreased Protein S concentration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* Resistance to protein C [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	* Decreased fibrinolysis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Clotting parameters**:
  * Activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT), prothrombin time (PT), thrombin time (TT), and international normalized ratio (INR) remain normal in absence of anticoagulants or coagulopathy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 4.3.4. PLATELETS AND WHITE BLOOD CELLS

* **Platelet count**:
  * Decreases due to increased consumption and hemodilution in third trimester although absolute production increases [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Most remain within normal limits [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Lower values (100-150 × 10^9/L) seen in 5-10% of healthy pregnancies at term [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Platelet count falls gradually throughout pregnancy; at term, the lower limit of a normal platelet count is 115 × 10^9/L [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Decreased platelet count is due to both haemodilution and shorter platelet lifespan [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **White blood cell count**:
  * Increases during pregnancy until after delivery (inflammatory state of pregnancy) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Normal range: 8,000-10,000/mm³ [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 May mask signs of sepsis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * WCC is elevated in pregnancy due to an increase in neutrophils and monocytes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Normal range in pregnancy is often taken as 6,000-16,000/mm³ from the 12th gestational week [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * WCC may increase to as much as 30,000/mm³ during labor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * NUH threshold is for 20 and above for CNB

| Parameter                        | Change    | Percentage |
| -------------------------------- | --------- | ---------- |
| **Increased**                    |           |            |
| Red cell mass                    | Increased | -          |
| WCC                              | Increased | -          |
| Factors I, VII, VIII, IX, X, XII | Increased | -          |
| ESR/CRP                          | Increased | -          |
| Fibrin degradation products      | Increased | -          |
| **Decreased**                    |           |            |
| Hb                               | Decreased | -          |
| Hct                              | Decreased | -          |
| Platelet                         | Decreased | 5%         |
| Albumin                          | Decreased | 25%        |
| Pseudocholinesterase             | Decreased | 20-30%     |
| Factor XI, XIII                  | Decreased | -          |
| PT+PTT                           | Decreased | 20%        |
| **Unchanged**                    |           |            |
| Bleeding time                    | Unchanged | -          |
| Factor II, VII                   | Unchanged | -          |
### 4.3.5. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* Uterine contraction at delivery returns approximately 500 mL to circulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]]:
  * ⚠️ May delay signs and symptoms of major hemorrhage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* Hypercoagulable state increases risk of thromboembolism [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* Anemia reduces oxygen-carrying capacity and narrows the margin of safety for blood loss [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* Pregnant patients are at significantly increased risk of venous thromboembolism (4-5× higher risk in pregnancy) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
* Blood loss during NVD and early puerperium ~600ml

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy creates a hypervolemic, relatively anemic, and hypercoagulable state. Blood volume increases by 40%, with plasma volume expansion exceeding red cell mass increase, resulting in physiologic anemia. Coagulation system changes include increased procoagulant factors and decreased anticoagulant activity, creating a prothrombotic state. The increased blood volume helps prepare for blood loss at delivery, while coagulation changes reduce hemorrhage risk but increase thromboembolism risk. These changes have important implications for fluid management, transfusion thresholds, and venous thromboembolism prophylaxis.

## 4.4. GASTROINTESTINAL SYSTEM

### 4.4.1. ANATOMICAL CHANGES

* **Stomach displacement**:
  * Upward displacement by gravid uterus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Altered axis and increased intragastric pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * The gravid uterus displaces the stomach and diaphragm upwards, reducing the acute angle of the oesophagus as it passes through the diaphragm [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * In the third trimester, the gravid uterus increases gastric pressure, further reducing the lower oesophageal barrier pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .

### 4.4.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES

* **Esophageal changes**:
  * Lower esophageal sphincter (LOS) tone decreases due to progesterone-induced muscle relaxation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Displacement of intra-abdominal segment of esophagus into thorax [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Progesterone-induced smooth muscle relaxation decreases the tone of the LOS, leading to sphincter incompetence [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Esophageal and intestinal transit is slowed by progesterone
* **Gastric emptying**:
  * Not significantly delayed in nonlaboring patients [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Delayed during labor [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Further delayed by systemic opioids for labor analgesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]], [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Epidural analgesia may facilitate gastric emptying due to better pain relief compared to labor without epidural analgesia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Gastric pH and secretion**:
  * The hormone gastrin is secreted by the placenta from the 15th week of gestation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] .
  * Compared with non-pregnant patients, gastric volume is increased and gastric pH decreased [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] .
  * Aspiration causes a greater degree of lung injury due to lower pH [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] .
* **Return to normal**:
  * Gastrointestinal changes return to prepregnancy state by 24-48 hours postpartum [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].

### 4.4.3. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* **Increased aspiration risk** due to:
  * Increased intragastric pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Reduced LOS tone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Delayed gastric emptying during labor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Preventive strategies**:
  * ⚠️ Consider all pregnant women after first trimester to have full stomachs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Pharmacologic interventions for scheduled procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]]:
	* H2-receptor antagonists
	* Proton pump inhibitors (e.g., omeprazole)
	* Metoclopramide
  * For emergency cesarean section [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]]:
	* Sodium citrate to neutralize gastric acid
	* Rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure for general anesthesia
  * NPO (nil per os) recommendations for elective procedures similar to non-pregnant patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Anesthetic implications**:
  * Gastro-oesophageal reflux commonly causes heartburn in pregnancy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
  * Major anaesthetic implication is increased risk of Mendelson's syndrome (pneumonitis from pulmonary aspiration of acidic gastric contents) under general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
  * Rapid sequence induction and non-particulate antacids are indicated for general anesthesia in pregnant patients beyond the first trimester [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy significantly increases aspiration risk due to anatomical displacement of the stomach, reduced lower esophageal sphincter tone, and delayed gastric emptying during labor. These changes mandate aspiration prophylaxis and appropriate anesthetic techniques (e.g., rapid sequence induction) when general anesthesia is required. Gastroesophageal reflux is common during pregnancy, with clinical manifestations like heartburn, but the most serious concern is the increased risk of aspiration pneumonitis during general anesthesia.

## 4.5. RENAL SYSTEM

### 4.5.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES

* **Renal blood flow** increases by 60% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Renal vasculature vasodilation** occurs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Glomerular filtration rate (GFR)** increases by 60% by end of first trimester [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Laboratory value changes**:
  * Decreased serum creatinine, urea, and uric acid values [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * Normal serum creatinine in pregnancy: 0.5-0.6 mg/dL [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Values greater than 1.0 mg/dL warrant further investigation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Additional changes**:
  * Increased bicarbonate excretion to buffer respiratory alkalosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * Small amount of proteinuria and glycosuria may be normal [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * Reduced plasma osmolarity due to water retention from increased RAAS activation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * Anatomical changes [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] 
	* Dilatation of ureters and renal pelvis from mechanical obstruction by the gravid uterus and progesterone-induced smooth muscle dilatation
	* These changes make urinary tract infection and pyelonephritis more common in pregnancy
  * Glycosuria: Tubular reabsorption of glucose at proximal tubules beyond max reabsorption threshold cannot keep pace with the increase in GFR, making urinary dipstick testing an unreliable test for diabetes mellitus in pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] .
  * Proteinuria: Tubular protein reabsorption mechanisms are insufficient to match the increased GFR. Upper limit of normal range is generally 300 mg of protein in 24-hour urine collection (compared with 150 mg in non-pregnant state) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] .

### 4.5.2. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* **Drug clearance**:
  * Increased clearance of renally eliminated drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * May require dose adjustments for certain medications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **UTI risk**:
  * Increased risk due to progesterone causing ureteric smooth muscle relaxation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
  * Uterus can compress ureters in later pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]].
* **Uric acid levels** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.3 Renal, Cellular, Body Fluids and Electrolytes, Acid Base|ⓘ]] 
  * In early pregnancy, increased GFR results in lower uric acid concentration
  * In third trimester, uric acid concentration steadily increases above the pre-pregnancy level due to increased tubular reabsorption
  * Hyperuricaemia in pregnancy is associated with pre-eclampsia
* Increased urinary frequency due to progesterone induced relaxation of bladder muscle

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy significantly increases renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate, resulting in decreased serum creatinine and increased clearance of renally eliminated drugs. Anatomical changes include dilatation of ureters and renal pelvis. Normal laboratory values differ from non-pregnant values, and mild proteinuria or glycosuria may be physiologic. These changes have implications for drug dosing, interpretation of laboratory values, and increased susceptibility to urinary tract infections.

## 4.6. HEPATIC SYSTEM

### 4.6.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES

* **Pseudocholinesterase activity**:
  * Lower during early pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Maximal decrease on third postpartum day [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * 20-33% lower than non-pregnant state [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Due to hemodilution and reduced synthesis by liver [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Plasma cholinesterase concentration decreases by 25% due to both decreased hepatic synthesis and increased plasma volume [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Enzyme levels**:
  * Increased alkaline phosphatase (3 times normal due to placental production) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Levels of glutamyltransferase, alanine aminotransferase and lactic acid dehydrogenase are slightly elevated [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * ALP originates from both liver and placenta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Protein synthesis**:
  * Hepatic protein production doesn't keep pace with plasma volume increase [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Albumin concentration decreases throughout pregnancy, from around 35 g/L to 25 g/L [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Other changes**:
  * Increased gallstone frequency due to increased bile secretion, cholesterol and gallbladder hypomotility (progesterone) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Clinical signs usually associated with chronic liver disease (e.g., palmar erythema, spider naevi) may occur normally in pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .

### 4.6.2. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS

* **Succinylcholine dosing**:
  * Dose adjustment generally not indicated despite reduced pseudocholinesterase activity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
	* Activity can be reduced by 50% before increased sensitivity occurs [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Exception: Patients with HELLP syndrome (hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, low platelets) may have significantly reduced pseudocholinesterase activity requiring dose adjustment [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Suxamethonium is metabolised by plasma cholinesterase; decreased enzyme activity leads to prolonged suxamethonium action [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Drug metabolism**:
  * Potential changes in metabolism of hepatically cleared drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy decreases pseudocholinesterase activity by 20-33%, though rarely to a clinically significant degree except in specific conditions like HELLP syndrome. Elevated alkaline phosphatase is normal due to placental production and should not be misinterpreted as liver dysfunction. Albumin concentration decreases, which can affect drug binding. These changes generally have minimal impact on anesthetic management except in patients with underlying liver disease or specific conditions like HELLP syndrome.

## 4.7. ENDOCRINE SYSTEM

### 4.7.1. MAJOR HORMONAL CHANGES

* **Placental hormones** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] 
  * **β-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG)**:
	* Secreted by placenta shortly after implantation
	* Detectable from second week of pregnancy
	* Levels double every 2 days until peak at 10 weeks gestation
	* Main role is to prolong life of corpus luteum, which secretes progesterone and estrogen
	* May help suppress maternal immune response, protecting placenta and embryo
  * **Human placental lactogen (hPL)**:
	* Structure similar to growth hormone
	* Secreted by syncytiotrophoblast cells
	* Concentration increases throughout pregnancy, peaking near term
	* Functions:
	  * Increases maternal lipolysis, increasing availability of free fatty acids
	  * Decreases maternal peripheral insulin sensitivity
	  * Stimulates breast growth and development
  * **Progesterone**:
	* Secreted by corpus luteum in early pregnancy and placenta in second/third trimesters
	* Functions:
	  * Prepares and promotes endometrial growth
	  * Uterine muscle relaxation, suppressing contractions
	  * Forms cervical mucus plug, protecting from infection
	  * Develops milk glands for lactation
  * **Estrogen**:
	* Three types synthesized by placenta: estradiol, estrone, oestriol
	* Oestriol is main pregnancy estrogen, produced from foetal adrenal precursor
	* Functions:
	  * Increases uteroplacental blood flow
	  * Stimulates uterine growth
	  * Sensitizes myometrium to oxytocin before labor
	  * Increases thromboembolic risk

### 4.7.2. THYROID CHANGES

* **Early pregnancy changes**:
  * Increasing levels of human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG) activate thyroid stimulating hormone receptors [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] due to common alpha-subunit w TSH
  * Can lead to follicular hyperplasia, increased vascularity and transient hyperthyroidism [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Later pregnancy**:
  * Increased thyroxine and T3 levels [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Enlarged pituitary [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanism** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] 
  * Oestriol stimulates liver to synthesize additional thyroxine-binding globulin (TBG)
  * Thyrotropin-releasing hormone secretion increases as a result of negative-feedback loop
  * Increased TSH stimulates thyroid to secrete additional T3 and T4 (free T4 is unchanged/decreased)

### 4.7.3. GLUCOSE METABOLISM

* **Insulin sensitivity**:
  * Relative resistance to insulin in late pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Compensatory increase in insulin synthesis and secretion [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Fetal aspects**:
  * Fetal insulin levels independent of maternal insulin production [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Dependent on maternal glucose, which crosses the placenta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Poor maternal glucose control increases risk of neonatal hypoglycemia after delivery [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * ⚠️ Chronic maternal hyperglycemia increases risk of macrosomia and shoulder dystocia [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Mechanisms**:
  * Human placental lactogen induces insulin resistance [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * This allows more glucose across the placenta into fetal circulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Insulin acts as a growth hormone in the fetus (diabetic mothers tend to have macrosomic babies) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 4.7.4. OTHER ENDOCRINE CHANGES

* **Prolactin (PRL)** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] 
  * Estrogen stimulates dramatic increase in pituitary secretion
  * Role is preparation of breasts for lactation
  * Pituitary gland doubles in size to accommodate extra lactotrophs
  * Increased metabolic demands make pituitary vulnerable to ischemia (Sheehan's syndrome in prolonged hypotension)
* **Parathyroid hormone (PTH)** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] 
  * Ca²⁺ is transferred across placenta to meet foetal requirements
  * Maternal parathyroid glands sense fall in plasma Ca²⁺ and increase PTH secretion
  * PTH increases plasma Ca²⁺ by increasing bone resorption, renal tubular Ca²⁺ reabsorption, and activation of vitamin D
  * Clinical significance: Pregnant patients on low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) for thromboembolism risk may experience worsened PTH-related reduction in bone mineral density
- **Basal Metabolic Rate**: increases by 15% at term

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy induces significant endocrine changes, particularly in thyroid function and glucose metabolism. The development of insulin resistance and increased insulin production is a normal adaptation that allows glucose transfer to the fetus but can unmask or exacerbate diabetes. Placental hormones (hCG, hPL, progesterone, estrogen) play crucial roles in maintaining pregnancy and preparing for lactation. These changes have implications for anesthetic management, particularly in patients with pre-existing endocrine disorders.

## 4.8. NEUROLOGICAL CHANGES [PART A/B]

Tags: #pA_physio #pB_C1 #pC_MK

Part A Reference: Neurological Physiology (Page 9)

Part B Reference: C1 Obstetric Anesthesia (Page 11)

Part C Reference: Medical Knowledge - Clinical Applications

### 4.8.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Neurological adaptations during pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications for neuraxial anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Knowledge integration for patient care [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High (Important for neuraxial techniques)

### 4.8.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES [PART A]

* **Epidural space**:
  * Reduced volume due to engorgement of epidural veins [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Increased sensitivity to local anesthetics (spinal LA dose reduces by about 25%) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Epidural pressure increased to around +1 cmH2O (compared to -1 cmH2O in non-pregnant state) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Due to engorgement of epidural veins from IVC compression [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * In first stage of labor, pressure increases to +4-10 cmH2O [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Bearing down in second stage can increase pressure to +60 cmH2O [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Epidural vein engorgement increases risk of accidental venous cannulation when placing catheter [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Cerebrospinal fluid**:
  * Cerebrospinal fluid pressure unchanged from pre-pregnancy value [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Cerebrospinal fluid pressure increases significantly in second stage of labor, up to 70 cmH2O [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Increased lumbar lordosis** and intra-abdominal pressure [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **General anesthetic sensitivity**:
  * Increased sensitivity to anesthetic agents [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Reduced minimum alveolar concentration (MAC) requirement by up to 30% [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * MAC of volatile anesthetics needed to prevent movement is reduced by 30-40% in pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Contributing factors to MAC reduction [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] 
	* Progesterone's sedative effects
	* β-endorphins (β-HCG) secreted by placenta throughout pregnancy, especially in labor
	* Other factors: human placental lactogen, estrogen, prolactin
	* Neural sensitivity to local anesthetics increased, explaining lower doses required for regional anesthesia

### 4.8.3. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS [PART B]

* **Regional anesthesia effects**:
  * Smaller doses of local anesthetic required for adequate block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Higher block levels may occur with standard doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Faster onset of both sensory and motor blockade [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Reduced doses help minimize hemodynamic effects while providing adequate analgesia/anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Anatomical considerations**:
  * Positioning more challenging due to increased lordosis and enlarged abdomen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Midline identification may be more difficult [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Sitting position often preferred for neuraxial technique placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Pregnancy alters neuraxial anatomy with engorgement of epidural veins and increased sensitivity to local anesthetics. These changes require dose adjustments for neuraxial anesthesia to prevent excessive spread while maintaining efficacy. Increased sensitivity to general anesthetics also necessitates dose reductions. The increased epidural pressure and engorgement of vessels increases the risk of accidental venous cannulation during epidural placement and contributes to the enhanced spread of local anesthetics.

## 4.9. MUSCULOSKELETAL CHANGES [PART A/B]

Tags: #pA_physio #pB_C1 #pC_MK

Part A Reference: Musculoskeletal Physiology (Page 9)

Part B Reference: C1 Obstetric Anesthesia (Page 11)

Part C Reference: Medical Knowledge - Clinical Applications

### 4.9.1. SYLLABUS CORRELATION

* **Part A:** Musculoskeletal adaptations during pregnancy [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Part B:** Clinical implications for patient positioning and care [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
* **Part C:** Knowledge integration for patient safety [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].

**Exam Weighting:** Low-Medium

### 4.9.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES [PART A]

* **Ligament laxity**:
  * Ligaments become increasingly lax as pregnancy progresses [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Due to placental secretion of the hormone relaxin [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Widened pubic symphysis.
* **Postural changes**:
  * Progressive lordosis to compensate for anterior weight of uterus.
  * Altered center of gravity.
* **Fat stores**: increases by 3kg in first half of pregnancy

### 4.9.3. CLINICAL IMPLICATIONS [PART B]

* **Positioning considerations**:
  * Increased attention to proper positioning during procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Careful padding and joint protection required during anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * Important to pay close attention to positioning during general anesthesia to minimize risk of post-operative back and joint problems [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]] .
* **Regional anesthesia effects**:
  * Changes in spinal curvature can affect spread of neuraxial anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].
  * May influence approach for neuraxial techniques [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Ligament laxity increases during pregnancy due to relaxin secretion, affecting joint stability and posture. These changes necessitate careful positioning during anesthesia to prevent postoperative musculoskeletal complaints. Postural changes, particularly increased lordosis, can influence the spread of neuraxial anesthetics and may affect technical approach for neuraxial procedures.

# 5. PLACENTAL TRANSFER AND FETAL EXPOSURE TO ANESTHETIC DRUGS
<!-- EPA-7:K2:foetal_neonatal_placental_physiology EPA-7:K3:applied_pharmacology_obstetric -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1. PHYSIOLOGY (~32% of Total Weightage)|Part A: Physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S3 Obstetrics Anaesthesia and Analgesia|Part B: Obstetrics]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: Medical Knowledge]]

## 5.1. PLACENTAL DRUG TRANSFER

### 5.1.1. PLACENTAL FUNCTIONS

* Acts as interface between mother and fetus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* Major functions include:
  * Gas exchange [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Transfer of nutrients and waste products between maternal and fetal plasma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Transfer of immunity (immunoglobulins) from mother to fetus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Secretion of hormones important for fetal growth and development [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].

### 5.1.2. MECHANISM OF DRUG TRANSFER

* Most anesthetic agents readily cross the placenta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
* **Primary transfer mechanisms** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Simple diffusion (most common for anesthetic agents) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	  * Oxygen
  * Facilitated diffusion using a carrier [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	  * Glucose
  * Active transport using adenosine triphosphate (ATP) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	  * Calcium, Amino acids
  * Pinocytosis [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
	  * IgG
* **Simple diffusion** follows **Fick's law** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Rate of diffusion (Q/t) = KA(Cm − Cf)/D
	* K = diffusion constant
	* A = surface area for exchange
	* Cm = concentration of free drug in maternal blood
	* Cf = concentration of free drug in fetal blood
	* D = thickness of diffusion barrier
  * 💡 The driving force is the concentration gradient of free drug between maternal and fetal plasma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Fick's law states that the rate of transfer across a membrane is proportional to the concentration gradient across that membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .

### 5.1.3. FACTORS AFFECTING PLACENTAL TRANSFER

* **Molecular weight** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * <500 Da: Readily diffuse across placenta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * 500-1,000 Da: More restricted transfer [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * 💡 Most anesthetic drugs have molecular weights <500 Da [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Graham's law states that the rate of passive diffusion is inversely proportional to the square root of the molecule's size [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Small molecules such as sevoflurane and propofol diffuse very quickly, especially those less than 600 daltons [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Lipid solubility** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Highly lipophilic drugs readily diffuse across biologic membranes, including placenta [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * More lipid soluble molecules are able to cross the membrane more readily [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Small, lipid soluble opioids are generally avoided in pregnancy for this reason [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Ionization** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Only nonionized form crosses placental membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Degree depends on pKa of drug and pH of maternal blood [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Governed by Henderson-Hasselbalch equation: pH = pKa + log(conjugate base/acid) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * **Ion trapping**: Lower pH in fetus can result in increased proportion of ionized drug, preventing back-diffusion to maternal circulation [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Fat soluble molecules move across membranes much more readily, however fat solubility only applies to the unionized fraction of a drug [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Since fetal blood is more acidic, basic drugs will be more ionized in fetal blood than maternal blood, which can lead to ion trapping [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Bupivacaine is a classic example as it is commonly used for spinal anesthesia in obstetrics. Risk is further increased in placental insufficiency as the fetus develops worsening acidosis, increasing degree of ion trapping and subsequent risk of toxicity [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Protein binding** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Rate of drug passage inversely proportional to protein binding [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * With sufficient time, highly protein-bound drugs (e.g., bupivacaine) can accumulate in fetus [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Only the 'free' fraction of drug in the blood can cross the cell membrane [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Highly protein bound drugs will tend to stay in the plasma [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
  * Protein binding in the fetal blood will encourage passage of drugs across the placenta, by reducing the free fraction available and therefore the concentration gradient [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]] .
* **Maternal factors** [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]]:
  * Administered dose [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
  * Mode and site of administration [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#2. PHARMACOLOGY + BIOSTATISTICS (~33% of Total Weightage)|ⓘ]].
